A First Order Theory of Diagram Chasing
Assia Mahboubi, Matthieu Piquerez

Abstract
This paper discusses the formalization of proofs “by diagram chasing”, a standard technique for proving properties in abelian categories. We discuss how the essence of diagram chases can be captured by a simple many-sorted first-order theory, and we study the models and decidability of this theory. The longer-term motivation of this work is the design of a computer-aided instrument for writing reliable proofs in homological algebra, based on interactive theorem provers.



Nantes Université, École Centrale Nantes, CNRS, INRIA, LS2N, UMR 6004, France0002-0312-5461
Nantes Université, École Centrale Nantes, CNRS, INRIA, LS2N, UMR 6004, France

ccs
Theory of computation Logic and verification

keywords
Diagram chasing, formal proofs, abelian categories, decidability

1
Introduction

Homological algebra
[
8
]
attaches and studies a sequence of algebraic objects, typically groups or modules, to a certain space, e.g., a ring or a topological space, in order to better understand the latter. In this field, diagram chasing is a major proof technique, which is usually carried out via a form of diagrammatic reasoning on abelian categories. A diagram can be seen as a functor
F\colon J\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, whose domain
J
, the indexing category, is a small category
[
12
]
. Diagrams are usually represented as directed multi-graphs, also called
quivers
, whose vertices are decorated with objects of
\mathcal{C}
, and arrows with morphisms. Paths in such graphs thus correspond to chains of composable arrows. Diagrams allow for visualizing the existence of certain morphisms, and to study identities between certain compositions of morphisms. In particular, a diagram
commutes
when any two paths with same source and target lead to identical composite. For instance, the commutativity of the following diagram:
.
.
.
b
c
a

asserts that morphism
a
is equal to the composition of morphisms
c
and
b
, denoted
b\circ c
. Commutativity of diagrams in certain categories can be used to state more involved properties, and diagram chasing essentially consists in establishing the existence, injectivity, surjectivity of certain morphisms, or the exactness of some sequences, using hypotheses of the same nature. The
five lemma
or the
snake lemma
are typical examples of proofs “by diagram chasing”, also called diagram chases. On paper, diagrams help conveying in a convincing manner proofs otherwise consisting of overly pedestrian chains of equations. The tension between readability and elusiveness may however become a challenge. For instance, diagram chases may rely on non-trivial duality arguments, that is, on the fact that a property about diagrams in any abelian category remains true after reversing all the involved arrows, although the replay of a given proof
mutatis mutandis
cannot be fulfilled in general.

Motivated in part by the second author’s experience in writing intricate diagram chases (see for instance
[
11
, p.337]
), this work aims at laying the foundations of a computer-aided instrument for writing reliable proofs in homological algebra, based on interactive theorem provers. The present article discusses the design of a formal language for statements of properties amenable to proofs by diagram chasing, according to three objectives. The first is
simplicity and expressivity
: this language should be at the same time simple enough to be implemented in a formal library, and expressive enough to encompass the desired corpus of results. Then,
duality
arguments in proofs shall follow directly from a meta-property of the language. Finally, the corresponding proof system should allow for
effective
proofs of commutativity clauses, that is, proving that the commutativity of some diagram follows from the commutativity of some other diagrams, so that these proofs can eventually be automated.

Definition 1
.

We define the many-sorted signatures
\mathring{\Sigma}
, resp.
\Sigma
, as follows: the sorts of signature
\mathring{\Sigma}
, resp.
\Sigma
, are finite, resp. acyclic finite, quivers. The symbols of
\mathring{\Sigma}
, resp.
\Sigma
, consists of one function symbol
\mathrm{restr}_{m:Q^{\prime}\to Q}
, of arity
Q\to Q^{\prime}
, per each quiver morphism, resp. each embedding between acyclic quivers,
m
and one predicate
\mathrm{commute}_{Q}
on sort
Q
for each finite, resp. acyclic finite, quiver
Q
.

The thesis of the present article is that signature
\Sigma
fulfills the three above objectives. We validate this thesis by giving a first-order theory for diagrams over small and abelian small categories respectively. We state and prove a duality theorem and motivate the choice of
\Sigma
over the possibly more intuitive
\mathring{\Sigma}
by the effectiveness objective. The rest of the article is organized as follows. We first fix some vocabulary and notations in
\cref
sec:prelim, so as in particular to make
\cref
def:sig precise. Then,
\cref
sec:small introduces a theory for small categories and describes its models,
\cref
sec:dual discusses duality, before
\cref
sec:abel provides an analogue study for abelian categories. Last, we prove in
\cref
sec:dec the decidability, resp. undecidability, of commutativity clauses in
\Sigma
, resp.
\mathring{\Sigma}
, before concluding in
\cref
sec:concl.

2
Preliminaries

In all what follows,
\mathbb{N}:=\{0,1,\dots\}
refers to the set of non-negative integers. If if
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
[k]
denotes the finite collection
\{0,\dots,k-1\}
. We denote
\mathrm{card}A
the cardinal of a finite set
A
. We use the notation
\mathrm{id}
for the identity map.

2.1
Quivers

Definition 2

(General quiver, dual)
.

A
general quiver

\mathcal{Q}
is a quadruple
(V_{\mathcal{Q}},A_{\mathcal{Q}},s_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon A_{\mathcal{Q}}\to V_{\mathcal{Q}},t_{\mathcal{Q}}\colon A_{\mathcal{Q}}\to V_{\mathcal{Q}})
where
V_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
A_{\mathcal{Q}}
are two sets. The element of
V_{\mathcal{Q}}
are called the
vertices
of
\mathcal{Q}
and the element of
A_{\mathcal{Q}}
are called
arrows
. If
a\in A_{\mathcal{Q}}
,
s_{\mathcal{Q}}(a)
is called the
source
of
a
and
t_{\mathcal{Q}}(a)
is called its
target
.
The
dual
of a quiver
\mathcal{Q}
is the quiver
\mathcal{Q}^{\dagger}\coloneqq(V_{\mathcal{Q}},A_{\mathcal{Q}},t_{\mathcal{Q}},s_{\mathcal{Q}})
, which swaps the source and the target maps of
\mathcal{Q}
.

Definition 3

(Morphism, embedding, restriction)
.

A
morphism of quiver

m\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
, is the data of two maps
m_{V}\colon V_{\mathcal{Q}}\to V_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
and
m_{A}\colon A_{\mathcal{Q}}\to A_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
such that
m_{A}\circ s_{\mathcal{Q}}=s_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\circ m_{A}
and
m_{A}\circ t_{\mathcal{Q}}=t_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}\circ m_{A}
. Such a morphism is called an
embedding of quivers
if moreover both
m_{V}
and
m_{A}
are injective. In this case we write
m\colon\mathcal{Q}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
.

If
A
is a subset of
A_{\mathcal{Q}}
, the
(spanning) restriction of
\mathcal{Q}
to
A
denoted
\mathcal{Q}|_{A}
is the quiver
(V_{\mathcal{Q}},A,s_{\mathcal{Q}}|_{A},t_{\mathcal{Q}}|_{A})
. There is a canonical embedding
\mathcal{Q}|_{A}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
.

We denote by
\varnothing
the empty quiver with no vertex and no
arrow, and by
\mathring{S}
the set of quivers
Q
such that
V_{Q}
and
A_{Q}
are finite subsets of
\mathbb{N}
. In this article, a
quiver
refers to an element of
\mathring{S}
. We use a non-cursive
Q
for elements of
\mathring{S}
, and a cursive
\mathcal{Q}
for general quivers.

For the sake of readability, we use drawings to describe
some elements of
\mathring{S}
, as for instance:
.
.
.

For a quiver
Q
denoted by such a drawing, the convention is that

V_{Q}=[\mathrm{card}{V_{Q}}]
and
A_{Q}=[\mathrm{card}{A_{Q}}]
.
From left to right, the drawn vertices
correspond to
0,1,\dots,\mathrm{card}{V_{Q}}-1
. Arrows are then numbered by sorting pairs
(s_{Q},t_{Q})
in
increasing lexicographical order, as in:
.
.
.
0
1
2
0
1
2
3

We also use drawings to denote embeddings. The black part represents the domain of the morphism, the union of black and gray parts represents its codomain. Here is an example of an embedding of the quiver
.
.
into the quiver drawn above.
.
.
.

Definition 4

(Path-quiver)
.

The
path-quiver of length
k
, denoted
\mathrm{PQ}_{k}
, is the quiver
with
k+1
vertices and
k
arrows
([k+1],[k],\mathrm{id},(i\mapsto i+1))
.

A path-quiver can be drawn as:
.
.
.
.
\dots

with at least one vertex. Such a path-quiver is called
nontrivial
if it has at least two vertices.

If
0\leq k\leq l
are two integers, we denote by
\textsf{sp}_{k,l}\colon\mathrm{PQ}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PQ}_{l}
the leftmost embedding of
\mathrm{PQ}_{k}
into
\mathrm{PQ}_{l}
, i.e., such that
(\textsf{sp}_{k,l})_{V}(0)=0
. If
k
and
l
are clear from the context, we draw
\textsf{sp}_{k,l}
as
if
k\neq 0
and as
.
if
k=0
. Moreover, we denote by
\textsf{tp}_{k,l}\colon\mathrm{PQ}_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PQ}_{l}
the rightmost embedding of
\mathrm{PQ}_{k}
into
\mathrm{PQ}_{l}
, i.e., such that
(\textsf{tp}_{k,l})_{V}(k)=l
. The corresponding drawings are
and
.
. Moreover, if
P
is a nontrivial path-quiver, we define
\textsf{st}_{P}\colon\hbox to10.89pt{\vbox to3.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.59721pt\lower-1.77777pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
\nullfont

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.59721pt}{-0.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{

to be the embedding mapping the first vertex on the leftmost vertex of
P
and the second vertex on the rightmost vertex of
P
. We denote this embedding
.
.
.

If
\mathcal{Q}
is a general quiver, a morphism of the form
p\colon\mathrm{PQ}_{k}\to\mathcal{Q}
, for some
k
, is called a
path of
\mathcal{Q}
from
u
to
v
of length
k
, where
u\coloneqq p(0)
and
v\coloneqq p(k)
. Two paths
p_{1}\colon P_{1}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
,
p_{2}\colon P_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
of
\mathcal{Q}

have the same extremities
if
p_{1}\circ\textsf{st}_{P_{1}}=p_{2}\circ\textsf{st}_{P_{2}}
. We denote by
\mathcal{BP}_{\!\mathcal{Q}}
the set of pair of paths of
\mathcal{Q}
having the same extremities. Let
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
be another general quiver and
m\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
be a morphism. Then we define
m_{*}(p)\coloneqq m\circ p
.

A general quiver is
acyclic
if any path of this quiver is an embedding. The set of acyclic quivers in
\mathring{S}
is denoted by
S
.

Definition 5

(Free category)
.

For a general quiver
\mathcal{Q}
, the
free category over
\mathcal{Q}
, denoted
{\langle\mathcal{Q}\rangle}
is the category with objects
\mathrm{Ob}_{{\langle\mathcal{Q}\rangle}}=V_{\mathcal{Q}}
whose morphisms
\mathrm{Hom}_{{\langle\mathcal{Q}\rangle}}(u,v)
, for two vertices
u
and
v
are the paths from
u
to
v
. The identity map from
u
to
u
is the empty path, and the composition is defined as the concatenation of paths.

Note that a morphism
m
of quivers induces a functor between the corresponding categories that we denote by
\Phi_{m}
. In the other direction, any small category
\mathcal{C}
has an
underlying quiver
.

2.2
Diagrams

We can now introduce diagrams in a category, and a few useful specific examples thereof.

Definition 6

(Diagram)
.

For any category
\mathcal{C}
and any quiver
Q
, a
diagram in
\mathcal{C}
over
Q
is a functor from
{\langle Q\rangle}
to
\mathcal{C}
.

Let
P
be a path-quiver from vertex
u
to vertex
v
. To a diagram
D\colon{\langle P\rangle}\to\mathcal{C}
over
P
one can associate the corresponding composition of morphisms in the category
\mathcal{C}
, which is an element of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(D(u),D(v))
. We denote this element
\mathrm{comp}(D)
. By convention, when the path-quiver
P
is trivial,
\mathrm{comp}(D)
is the identity map
\mathrm{id}_{D(u)}
.

Definition 7

(Pullback)
.

Let
Q,Q^{\prime}
be two quivers and let
D
be a diagram over
Q
. Let
m\colon Q^{\prime}\to Q
be a morphism of quivers. We define the
pullback of
D
by
m
, denoted
m^{*}(D)
, as the diagram
D\circ\Phi_{m}
over
Q^{\prime}
.

Definition 8

(Commutative diagram)
.

For any category
\mathcal{C}
and any quiver
Q
,a diagram
D
over
Q
is
commutative
if
\mathrm{comp}(p_{1}^{*}(D))
and
\mathrm{comp}(p_{2}^{*}(D))
coincide for any two paths
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
in
Q
with same extremities, that is:

\forall(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathcal{BP}_{\!Q},\quad\mathrm{comp}(p_{1}^{*}(D))=\mathrm{comp}(p_{2}^{*}(D)).

(1)

The next lemma allows to reduce the number of distinct diagrams involved in a formula.

Lemma 9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be some category. Consider two diagrams
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
over
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, respectively. If the pullback of
D_{1}
by
m_{1}
coincides with the pullback of
D_{2}
by
m_{2}
, i.e.,

m^{*}_{1}(D_{1})=m^{*}_{2}(D_{2})

then, there exists a unique diagram
D^{\prime}
over
Q^{\prime}
such that
D_{1}
(resp.
D_{2}
) is the pullback of
D^{\prime}
by
m^{\prime}_{1}
(resp.
m^{\prime}_{2}
), i.e.,

D_{1}=m^{\prime*}_{1}(D^{\prime})\quad\textrm{and}\quad D_{2}=m^{\prime*}_{2}(D^{\prime})

Proof.

Immediate. ∎

Definition 10

(Pushout)
.

Consider four quivers
Q,Q_{1},Q_{2},Q^{\prime}
and four maps
m_{1}\colon Q\to Q_{1}
,
m_{2}\colon Q\to Q_{2}
,
m_{1}^{\prime}\colon Q_{1}\to Q^{\prime}
and
m_{2}^{\prime}\colon Q_{2}\to Q^{\prime}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{2}}

This data is a
pushout configuration
if:

•

m^{\prime}_{1}\circ m_{1}=m^{\prime}_{2}\circ m_{2}
,

•

Q^{\prime}=\mathrm{Im}(m^{\prime}_{1})\cup\mathrm{Im}(m^{\prime}_{2})
, (i.e.,
V_{Q^{\prime}}=\mathrm{Im}(m^{\prime}_{1,V})\cup\mathrm{Im}(m^{\prime}_{2,V})
and
A_{Q^{\prime}}=\mathrm{Im}(m^{\prime}_{1,A})\cup\mathrm{Im}(m^{\prime}_{2,A})
),

•

\mathrm{Im}(m^{\prime}_{1}\circ m_{1})=\mathrm{Im}(m^{\prime}_{1})\cap\mathrm{Im}(m^{\prime}_{2})
.

Consider a pushout configuration as in
\cref
def:pushout. The triple
(Q^{\prime},m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime})
is called a
pushout of
(Q,Q_{1},Q_{2},m_{1},m_{2})
. Such a pushout always exists, and any two such pushouts are isomorphic. Moreover, if
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are embeddings, then so are
m^{\prime}_{1}
and
m^{\prime}_{2}
.

Figure 1:
A pushout
Q^{\prime}
of two path-quivers
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
with respect to
\textsf{st}_{P_{1}}
and
\textsf{st}_{P_{2}}
.

Lemma 11
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be some category. Consider two diagrams
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
over
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, respectively. If the pullback of
D_{1}
by
m_{1}
coincides with the pullback of
D_{2}
by
m_{2}
, i.e.,

m^{*}_{1}(D_{1})=m^{*}_{2}(D_{2})

then there exists a unique diagram
D^{\prime}
over
Q^{\prime}
such that
D_{1}
(resp.
D_{2}
) is the pullback of
D^{\prime}
by
m^{\prime}_{1}
(resp.
m^{\prime}_{1}
), i.e.,

D_{1}=m^{\prime*}_{1}(D^{\prime})\quad\textrm{and}\quad D_{2}=m^{\prime*}_{2}(D^{\prime})

Proof.

Immediate. ∎

2.3
Category relations, path relations and quotient categories

We first name relations on the morphisms of a category that are compatible with composition:

Definition 12

(Category relation)
.

A
category relation
r
on
\mathcal{C}
is by definition the data of an equivalence relation
r_{A,B}\subseteq\mathrm{Hom}(A,B)^{2}
for any pair of objects
A
and
B
such that, for any objects
A,B,C
and any morphisms
f,g\in\mathrm{Hom}(A,B)
and
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Hom}(B,C)
,

f\sim g\ \text{ and }\ f^{\prime}\sim g^{\prime}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad f^{\prime}\circ f\sim g^{\prime}\circ g,

where we write
h\sim h^{\prime}
if
h
and
h^{\prime}
are in relation, i.e.,

(h,h^{\prime})\in r_{A,B}
. Such a relation is said
complete
if

r_{A,B}=\mathrm{Hom}(A,B)^{2}
for any pair of objects
A
and

B
.

Lemma 13

(Quotient category)
.

Given such a category relation, we define the
quotient category
\mathcal{C}/r
given by
\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}/r}:=\mathrm{Ob}_{\mathcal{C}}
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}/r}(A,B)=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)/r_{A,B}
is indeed a category for the induced composition.

Proof.

Immediate.
∎

We now name the relations between the paths of a general quiver induced by the corresponding identities of morphism composites, in the associated free category.

Definition 14
.

A
relation between paths with same extremities in
\mathcal{Q}
is by definition a subset of
\mathcal{BP}_{\!\mathcal{Q}}
. If
r\subseteq\mathcal{BP}_{\!\mathcal{Q}}
is such a relation then, for
(p,q)\in\mathcal{BP}_{\!\mathcal{Q}}
, we write
p\sim q
if
(p,q)\in r
. The complete path relation on
\mathcal{Q}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{BP}_{\!\mathcal{Q}}
, is denoted
\mathrm{tot}_{\mathcal{Q}}
. Note that
\mathcal{BP}_{\!\mathcal{Q}}=\bigsqcup_{A,B\in\mathrm{Ob}_{{\langle\mathcal{Q}\rangle}}}\mathrm{Hom}_{{\langle\mathcal{Q}\rangle}}(A,B)^{2}
. Such a relation
r
is called a
path relation
if it is a category relation on
{\langle\mathcal{Q}\rangle}
.

For instance, the equality of compositions in a small category
\mathcal{C}
induces a path relation on the underlying quiver
\mathcal{Q}
. If
r_{1},\dots,r_{l}
are some relations between paths with same extremities, we denote by
(r_{1},\dots,r_{l})
the smallest path relation containing
r_{1}
, …,
r_{l}
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
be another general quiver and let
m\colon\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
be a morphism. If
r\subseteq\mathcal{BP}_{\!\mathcal{Q}}
, we denote by
m_{*}(r)
the relation induced by the image by
m
of
r
in
\mathcal{BP}_{\!\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
.

2.4
Many-sorted logic, categorical interpretation

We first recall a few basic definitions mostly pertaining to many-sorted logic, applied to the signatures introduced by
\cref
def:sig, and we set the corresponding notations.

Let us first fix a countable set
X
, so that for each quiver
Q
in
\mathring{S}
(resp. in
S
), elements of the set
X_{Q}:=X\times\{Q\}
are the
variables of sort
Q
. A
term
of sort
Q
either is a variable of sort
Q
or has the form
\mathrm{restr}_{m\colon Q^{\prime}\to Q}(t)
, with
t
a term of sort
Q
. When possible, we leave the sorts implicit and simplify the notation of symbol
\mathrm{restr}_{m\colon Q^{\prime}\to Q}(t)
into
\mathrm{restr}_{m}
.

We denote the equality symbols by
\approx
. An
atom
is thus of the form
s\approx t
with
s
and
t
two terms of the same sort, or of the form
\mathrm{commute}_{Q}(t)
with
t
a term of sort
Q
. We consider first-order many-sorted formulas and write the sort of quantifiers as a subscript, i.e.,
\exists_{Q}x_{Q},\phi
and
\forall_{Q}x_{Q},\phi
where
Q\in\mathring{S},x_{Q}\in X_{Q}
and
\phi
is a formula. In what follows, we however drop sort subscripts when they are clear from the context.

We write
\exists!y,P(y)
for formula
\bigl(\exists y,\ P(y)\bigr)\wedge\bigl(\forall y_{1},y_{2},\ P(y_{1})\wedge P(y_{2})\to y_{1}\approx y_{2}\bigr)
. A formula with free variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
of respective sorts
Q_{1},\dots,Q_{n}
is said to be of
arity

Q_{1}\times\dots Q_{n}
.

Definition 15

(Models)
.

Let
Y\subseteq X\times\mathring{S}
be a set of variables. An
interpretation
(also called a
model
)
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathring{\Sigma}
over
Y
is a map such that

•

each sort
Q
is mapped to a domain set, denoted
\mathcal{M}_{Q}
,

•

each variable
x\in Y
of sort
Q
is mapped to an element
x^{\mathcal{M}}\in\mathcal{M}_{Q}
,

•

each function
\mathrm{restr}_{m\colon Q^{\prime}\rightarrow Q}
is mapped to function
\mathrm{restr}^{\mathcal{M}}_{m\colon Q^{\prime}\to Q}\colon\mathcal{M}_{Q}\to\mathcal{M}_{Q^{\prime}}
,

•

each predicate
\mathrm{commute}_{Q}
is mapped to a subset
\mathrm{commute}^{\mathcal{M}}_{Q}
of
\mathcal{M}_{Q}
.

An interpretation is extended in the usual way to all formulas whose free variables are in
Y
. An interpretation
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathring{\Sigma}
is an interpretation over the empty set.

We define interpretations (models) of
\Sigma
in a similar fashion.

The
evaluation of a formula
\phi
under an interpretation
\mathcal{M}
is either true or false depending on the truthiness of the interpretation of the formula. If
\phi
has no free variable, we write
\mathcal{M}\models\phi
if
\phi^{\mathcal{M}}
is true.

A
theory
is a set of formulas for a certain signature. Remember that signature
\Sigma
only differs from
\mathring{\Sigma}
by restricting the allowed sorts (to the acyclic quivers). If
\mathring{T}
is a theory for
\mathring{\Sigma}
, we thus define the
restriction of
\mathring{T}
to
\Sigma
, denoted
\mathring{T}|_{\Sigma}
, as the subset of formulas of
\mathring{T}
which are well-formed with respect to
\Sigma
. A
model of a theory
is a model such that the interpretation of every formula of the theory is true.

The prototypical models of the signatures introduced in
\cref
def:sig are actually diagrams over a certain category.

Definition 16

(Categorical interpretation)
.

To each small category
\mathcal{C}
, we associate an interpretation of
\Sigma
, resp.
\mathring{\Sigma}
, that we also denote by
\mathcal{C}
, as follows.

•

To each sort
Q
we associate the set
\mathcal{C}_{Q}
of diagrams in
\mathcal{C}
over
Q
.

•

\mathrm{restr}_{m}
is interpreted as the function mapping a diagram
D
to the diagram
m^{*}(D)
.

•

\mathrm{commute}^{\mathcal{C}}_{Q}
is the set of commutative diagrams in
\mathcal{C}
over
Q
.

We call such an interpretation a
categorical interpretation of
\Sigma
, resp.
\mathring{\Sigma}
.

3
A theory for diagrams over small categories

This section introduces a theory whose models of can be seen as categorical
interpretations.

3.1
Axioms

We now introduce the different axioms of the theory. A formula
F
with free variables
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
is written
F(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
so as to clarify the sorts of each variable in the arity of
F
.

Existence and uniqueness of the empty diagram

\mathrm{EmptyEU}\colon\qquad\exists!_{\varnothing}x,\quad x\approx x

Compatibility of restrictions

For any quivers
Q,Q^{\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}
and morphisms
m\colon Q\to Q^{\prime}
and
m^{\prime}\colon Q^{\prime}\to Q^{\prime\prime}
, we define:

\mathrm{RestrComp}_{m,m^{\prime}}\colon\qquad\forall_{Q^{\prime\prime}}x^{\prime\prime},\quad\mathrm{restr}_{m}(\mathrm{restr}_{m^{\prime}}(x^{\prime\prime}))\approx\mathrm{restr}_{m^{\prime}\circ m}(x^{\prime\prime}).

Pushout

For any pushout configuration as in
\cref
def:pushout, and using the same notations as this definition, we define the following formulas of arity
Q_{1}\times Q_{2}\times Q^{\prime}
:

\mathrm{Cospan}_{m^{\prime}_{1},m^{\prime}_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2},x^{\prime})\colon\qquad\mathrm{restr}_{m^{\prime}_{1}}(x^{\prime})\approx x_{1}\ \wedge\ \mathrm{restr}_{m^{\prime}_{2}}(x^{\prime})\approx x_{2}.

\displaystyle\mathrm{PushoutEU}_{m_{1},m_{2},m^{\prime}_{1},m^{\prime}_{2}}\colon\qquad\forall x_{1},x_{2},\quad

\displaystyle\mathrm{restr}_{m_{1}}(x_{1})\approx\mathrm{restr}_{m_{2}}(x_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\to\ \exists!\,x^{\prime},\ \mathrm{Cospan}_{m^{\prime}_{1},m^{\prime}_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2},x^{\prime}).

Composition

\mathrm{CompE}\colon\qquad\forall x,y,\exists z,\quad\mathrm{Comp}(x,y,z).

Equality of nontrivial paths

For any two nontrivial path-quivers
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, and
Q^{\prime}
,
m^{\prime}_{1}
,
m^{\prime}_{2}
such that the following diagram forms a pushout configuration (as for instance on
\cref
fig:pushout_path-quivers)
.
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{2}}

P_{1}\times P_{2}
:

Hence, we can see
\mathrm{EqPath}
as a relation with arity any pair of path-quivers. We ensure this relation to be an equivalence relation by defining for any path-quivers
P_{1},P_{2}
and
P_{3}
the three formulas
\mathrm{EqPathRefl}_{P_{1}}
,
\mathrm{EqPathSym}_{P_{1},P_{2}}
and
\mathrm{EqPathTrans}_{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}}
stating that the relation
\mathrm{EqPath}
is respectively reflexive, symmetric and transitive.

We also make sure to enforce the properties of a category relation. For this purpose, for any four path-quivers
P_{1}
,
P_{1}^{\prime}
,
P_{2}
, and
P_{2}^{\prime}
, of respective length
k_{1},k_{1}^{\prime},k_{2}
and
k_{2}^{\prime}
, we define the following formula where bound variables
x_{1},x_{2},x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2}
respectively have sort
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
,
P^{\prime}_{1}
and
P^{\prime}_{2}
and the sort of
x^{\prime\prime}_{i}
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, is
\mathrm{PQ}_{k_{i}+k^{\prime}_{i}}
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{EqPathConcat}_{P_{1},P_{2},P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2}}\colon\qquad\forall x_{1},x_{2},x^{\prime}_{1},x^{\prime}_{2},

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.73 -1.57 C 7.53 -4.35 12.15 -4.35 14.32 -3.1}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.86603}{0.5}{-0.5}{0.86603}{10.35033pt}{-2.23776pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.94 0 C 1.7 0.06 0.65 0.4 0 0.76 L 0 -0.76 C 0.65 -0.4 1.7 -0.06 1.94 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(x).

For any quiver
Q
, the following formula provides an analogue of the notion of commutativity of diagrams given in
\cref
ssec:diagram_commutativity,
\cref
eqn:diagram_commutativity:

\mathrm{PathCom}_{Q}\colon\qquad\forall_{Q}x,\bigwedge_{(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathcal{BP}_{\!Q}}\hskip-16.00008pt\mathrm{EqPath}(\mathrm{restr}_{p_{1}}(x),\mathrm{restr}_{p_{2}}(x))\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\mathrm{commute}(x).

where we recall that
\mathcal{BP}_{\!Q}
denotes the set of pair of paths of
Q
having the same extremities.

Definition 17
.

Theory
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
, over signature
\mathring{\Sigma}
, consists of the following formulas:

•

\mathrm{EmptyEU}
,
\mathrm{CompE}
,
\mathrm{IdE}
,
\mathrm{ComEq}
,

•

\mathrm{RestrComp}_{m,m^{\prime}}
for any pair of maps
m
and
m^{\prime}
as in
\cref
sssec:restr_comp,

•

\mathrm{PushoutEU}_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{1}^{\prime},m_{2}^{\prime}}
for a pushout configuration as in
\cref
def:pushout,

•

\mathrm{EqPathRefl}_{P_{1}},\mathrm{EqPathSym}_{P_{1},P_{2}},\mathrm{EqPathTrans}_{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}},\mathrm{EqPathConcat}_{P_{1},P_{2},P_{3},P_{4}}
for any quadruple of path-quivers
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
and
P_{4}
,

•

\mathrm{PathCom}_{Q}
for any quiver
Q
.

Theory
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
is defined as the restriction of
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
to
\Sigma
.

3.2
Models

Models of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
, resp.
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
are in fact exactly what we have called categorical interpretations.

Theorem 18
.

Every categorical interpretation of
\Sigma
, resp.
\mathring{\Sigma}
, is a model of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
, resp.
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
. Moreover, any model
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
, resp.
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
, has an isomorphic categorical interpretation.

Proof.

We only prove the theorem for
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
, as the proof for
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
is similar.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a small category, a routine check shows that the associated model
\mathcal{C}
of
\Sigma
verifies the theory
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
. For instance,

•

the formulas
\mathrm{PushoutEU}
follows from the remark in
\cref
def:pushout,

•

\mathrm{IdE}
and
\mathrm{CompE}
come from the existence of the identity map and the existence of the composition respectively,

•

for two path-quivers
P_{1}
,
P_{2}
and two diagrams
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
over them,
\mathrm{EqPath}_{P_{1},P_{2}}(D_{1},D_{2})
is the relation
\mathrm{comp}(D_{1})=\mathrm{comp}(D_{2})
, which is a path relation by
\cref
sec:quotient_category,

•

\mathrm{ComEq}
and
\mathrm{PathCom}_{Q}
follow from
\cref
eqn:diagram_commutativity.

.

Moreover, when both statements hold, the element
\beta
is unique.

Proof.

The fact that the first point induces the second one follows directly from
\mathrm{RestrComp}
. Hence we focus on the other direction and on the uniqueness.

We proceed by induction on the structure of
Q
. If
Q=\varnothing
is empty, the second point holds trivially. The first point and the uniqueness follows from
\mathrm{EmptyEU}
.

Assume first that the lemma holds for some quiver
Q_{1}
with no arrow. Let
Q
be the quiver
Q_{1}
with an extra vertex
v_{0}
. Let
\beta_{V}
and
\beta_{A}
be two maps as in the statement of the lemma, and
\beta_{1,V}
the restriction of
\beta_{V}
to
Q_{1,V}
. We have the following pushout configuration:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varnothing}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{2}=v_{0}}

Point
2
holds trivially. Let us prove the existence and the uniqueness of
\beta
satisfying the property of Point
1
. By induction, we get a unique
\beta_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{Q_{1}}
compatible with
\beta_{1,V}
and
\beta_{A}
. Set
\beta_{2}\coloneqq\beta_{V}(v_{0})
. By
\mathrm{EmptyEU}
,
\mathrm{restr}^{\mathcal{M}}_{m_{1}}(\beta_{1})=\mathrm{restr}^{\mathcal{M}}_{m_{2}}(\beta_{2})
. Hence we can apply
\mathrm{PushoutEU}_{m_{1},m_{2},m^{\prime}_{1},m^{\prime}_{2}}
to get a unique element
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{Q}
such that
\mathrm{Cospan}^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta)
. Using
\mathrm{RestrComp}
and the induction hypothesis, we can check that for
\beta^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{Q}
, there is an equivalence between
\mathrm{Cospan}^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{restr}_{v}^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta^{\prime})=\beta_{V}(v)
for any
v\in V_{Q}
. This concludes the induction.

.
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{2}\coloneqq a_{0}}

Assume that Point
2
holds. By induction, we get a unique element
\beta_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{Q_{1}}
compatible with
\beta_{V}
and
\beta_{1,A}
. Set
\beta_{2}\coloneqq\beta_{A}(a_{0})
. We have already proven the lemma for the quiver
.
.
. Hence we deduce that

\mathrm{restr}^{\mathcal{M}}_{m_{1}}(\beta_{1})=\mathrm{restr}^{\mathcal{M}}_{m_{2}}(\beta_{2}).

We can apply
\mathrm{PushoutEU}
as before to get Point
1
as well as the uniqueness part. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Set
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq{\langle\mathcal{Q}\rangle}
. Thanks to
\cref
lem:pushout, to each acyclic quiver
Q
and to each element
\beta\in\mathcal{M}_{Q}
, we can associate a unique diagram
\widetilde{\Psi}(\beta)
in
\widetilde{C}
verifying:

•

for any
v\in V_{Q}
,
\widetilde{\Psi}(\beta)(v)=\mathrm{restr}_{v}^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta)
.

•

for any
a\in A_{Q}
,
\widetilde{\Psi}(\beta)(a)
is the path of length one with arrow
\mathrm{restr}_{a}^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta)
.

The image of
\widetilde{\Psi}
is exactly the set of diagrams whose morphisms are paths of lengths one.

There is another important map. Let
A
and
B
be two objects of
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, and let
p\in\mathrm{Hom}(A,B)
. Recall that
p
is just a path from
A
to
B
in
Q
. Let
k
be the length of
p
. By
\cref
lem:pushout, there exists a unique element
\Theta(p)\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{PQ}_{k}}
such that

•

for each
v\in V_{\mathrm{PQ}_{k}}
,
\mathrm{restr}_{v}^{\mathcal{M}}(\Theta(p))=p(v)
,

•

for each
a\in A_{\mathrm{PQ}_{k}}
,
\mathrm{restr}_{a}^{\mathcal{M}}(\Theta(p))=p(a)
.

Relation
\mathrm{EqPath}^{\mathcal{M}}
thus induces a relation
r
on morphisms of
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
. Moreover,
\mathrm{EqPathRefl}
,
\mathrm{EqPathSym}
,
\mathrm{EqPathTrans}
and
\mathrm{EqPathConcat}
, together with
\cref
lem:pushout, make
r
a category relation. We can hence define the category
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}/r
. Now
\widetilde{\Psi}
induces a map
\Psi\colon\mathcal{M}_{Q}\to\mathcal{C}_{Q}
for any quiver
Q
, and we claim that
\Psi
induces a model isomorphism between
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{C}
.

Let
Q
be any acyclic quiver. We first prove that
\Psi
is injective. Let
\beta,\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{Q}
such that
\Psi(\beta)=\Psi(\gamma)
. For any vertex
v\in V_{Q}
,
\widetilde{\Psi}(\beta)(v)=\widetilde{\Psi}(\gamma)(v)
, i.e.,
\mathrm{restr}_{v}^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta)=\mathrm{restr}_{v}^{\mathcal{M}}(\gamma)
. Let
a
be an arrow of
Q
. Then we have the relation
\widetilde{\Psi}(\beta)(a)\sim\widetilde{\Psi}(\gamma)(a)
. By definition of
\mathrm{EqPath}_{{\hbox to14.92pt{\vbox to2.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.77081pt\lower-1.06943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

, we validate the premise of
\mathrm{ComEq}
, and thus the equality
\widetilde{\Psi}(\beta)(a)=\widetilde{\Psi}(\gamma)(a)
, i.e.,
\mathrm{restr}_{a}^{\mathcal{M}}(\beta)=\mathrm{restr}_{a}^{\mathcal{M}}(\gamma)
. By the uniqueness part of
\cref
lem:pushout, we get
\beta=\gamma
.

We now consider the surjectivity of
\Psi
. It suffices to prove that any morphism
p\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(A,B)
is in relation via
r
to a path of length one. Indeed, in such a case, for any diagram
\widetilde{D}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
over
Q
, one can find another diagram
\widetilde{D}^{\prime}
over
Q
whose morphisms are path of size one and such that any morphism of
\widetilde{D}
is in relation with the corresponding morphism of
\widetilde{D}^{\prime}
. Hence the induced diagrams in
\mathcal{C}
are equal. Moreover,
\widetilde{D}^{\prime}
is in the image of
\widetilde{\Psi}
, and we would get the surjectivity.

. By the case

, we can find

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}
such that
\mathrm{EqPath}(\mathrm{restr}^{\mathcal{M}}_{m^{\prime}_{1}}(\beta),\gamma_{1})
. We set
\gamma_{2}=\mathrm{restr}^{\mathcal{M}}_{m^{\prime}_{2}}(\beta)
. In particular we have
\mathrm{EqPath}(\gamma_{2},\gamma_{2})
. By
\mathrm{EqPathConcat}
, we get
\mathrm{EqPath}(\beta,\gamma)
where
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{PQ}_{k-1}}
is such that
\mathrm{Cospan}_{m^{\prime}_{1},m^{\prime}_{2}}(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma)
, and the result follows.

We have shown that
\Psi
induces a bijection between the corresponding domains. In order to conclude the proof, it remains to prove that
\Psi
commutes with
\mathrm{restr}
and with
\mathrm{commute}
. The commutativity with
\mathrm{restr}
follows from the definition of
\Psi
and the formulas
\mathrm{RestrComp}
.

The compatibility with the predicate
\mathrm{commute}
can be reduced to the compatibility of
\mathrm{EqPath}
via the formula
\mathrm{PathCom}
. Let
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
be two path-quivers and
\beta_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{P_{1}}
and
\beta_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{P_{2}}
. These elements correspond to paths
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
in
\mathcal{Q}
. Using the definitions of the different elements, we get the following chain of equivalences:

\displaystyle\mathrm{EqPath}^{\mathcal{C}}(\Psi(\beta_{1}),\Psi(\beta_{2}))\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{comp}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Psi(\beta_{1}))=\mathrm{comp}_{\mathcal{C}}(\Psi(\beta_{2}))

\displaystyle\hskip 88.2037pt\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{comp}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(\widetilde{\Psi}(\beta_{1}))\sim\mathrm{comp}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}}(\widetilde{\Psi}(\beta_{2}))\Leftrightarrow p_{1}\sim p_{2}\Leftrightarrow\mathrm{EqPath}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2}).

This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

4
Duality

Signatures of
\cref
def:sig are tailored to enforce a built-in, therefore easy to prove, duality principle, which we now make precise. Recall from
\cref
def:quiv that duality is an involution on quivers, and also on acyclic quivers. We define the dual of a formula over
\mathring{\Sigma}
as follows:

•

if
m\colon Q\to Q^{\prime}
is a morphism, then
m^{\dagger}\colon Q^{\dagger}\to Q^{\prime\dagger}
is defined by
m^{\dagger}_{V}=m_{V}
and
m^{\dagger}_{A}=m_{A}
.

•

if
x=(\mathrm{x},Q)
is a variable in
X\times\mathring{S}
then
x^{\dagger}\coloneqq(\mathrm{x},Q^{\dagger})
,

•

(\mathrm{restr}_{m}(x))^{\dagger}\coloneqq\mathrm{restr}_{m^{\dagger}}(x^{\dagger})
and
(\mathrm{commute}_{Q}(x))^{\dagger}\coloneqq\mathrm{commute}_{Q^{\dagger}}(x^{\dagger})
,

•

(x\approx y)^{\dagger}\coloneqq x^{\dagger}\approx y^{\dagger}
,

•

(\forall_{Q}x,\phi)^{\dagger}\coloneqq\forall_{Q^{\dagger}}x^{\dagger},\phi^{\dagger}
and
(\exists_{Q}x,\phi)^{\dagger}\coloneqq\exists_{Q^{\dagger}}x^{\dagger},\phi^{\dagger}
,

•

(\phi\wedge\psi)^{\dagger}\coloneqq\phi^{\dagger}\wedge\psi^{\dagger}
, etc.

For
Y
a set of variables,
Y^{\star}
denotes

\{x^{\star}\mid x\in Y\}
. For any theory
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}
denotes
\{\phi^{\dagger}\mid\phi\in\mathcal{T}\}
.

For
\mathcal{M}
an interpretation of
\mathring{\Sigma}
over a set of variables
Y
, we define its dual model
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}
as:

•

\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}_{Q}\coloneqq\mathcal{M}_{Q^{\dagger}}
,

•

for
x\in Y^{\dagger}
,
x^{\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}}\coloneqq(x^{\dagger})^{\mathcal{M}}
.

•

\mathrm{restr}^{\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}}_{m}\coloneqq\mathrm{restr}^{\mathcal{M}}_{m^{\dagger}}
and
\mathrm{commute}^{\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}}_{Q}\coloneqq\mathrm{commute}^{\mathcal{M}}_{Q^{\dagger}}
,

The duality involution also restricts to formulas, theories and models over
\Sigma
.

Example 20
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a small category, then the dual interpretation
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
is isomorphic to the model of the dual category, both with respect to
\mathring{\Sigma}
and to
\Sigma
.

Theorem 21

(Duality theorem)
.

Let
\phi
be a formula with free variables included in
Y\subseteq X\times S
, resp. in
Y\subseteq X\times\mathring{S}
, and let
\mathcal{M}
be a model of
\Sigma
, resp. of
\mathring{\Sigma}
. Then

\mathcal{M}\models\phi\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}\models\phi^{\dagger}.

Proof.

More generally, a formula is provable if and only if its dual is provable.
∎

Remark 22
.

The duality principle has some useful direct consequences:

•

If
\phi
is a valid, resp. satisfiable, resp. unsatisfiable, formula, so is
\phi^{\dagger}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{T}
be theory such that any model of
\mathcal{T}
verifies
\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}
. If
\phi
is a valid, resp. satisfiable, resp. unsatisfiable, formula among models of
\mathcal{T}
, so is
\phi^{\dagger}
.

•

We have the following reciprocal. Let
\mathcal{T}
be a theory such that any model
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathcal{T}
verifies that
\mathcal{M}^{\dagger}\models\mathcal{T}
, then every model of
\mathcal{T}
verifies
\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}
.

The following fact follows directly from this last point,
\cref
thm:category_theory and
\cref
ex:dual_category.

Proposition 23
.

Models of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
verify
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}^{\dagger}
, and models of
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
verify
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}^{\dagger}
.

5
A theory for diagrams over abelian categories

We now introduce a theory whose models are diagrams in small abelian categories. We rely on the set of axioms given by Freyd in
[
5
]
. This reference is particularly well-suited for our purpose. Indeed, the author does not impose the homomorphisms between any two objects of an abelian category to form a group, but this fact rather follows from the axioms.

Let us first introduce common notions of category theory in our logic. Here is a formula of arity
.
.
which corresponds to monicity of a map in a category.

The dual formula is called
\mathrm{Epi}
.

Let
Q
be a quiver. We define the
cone of
Q
as the quiver

\mathrm{cone}(Q)\coloneqq(V_{Q}\sqcup\{v_{0}\},A_{Q}\sqcup\{a_{v}\mid v\in V_{Q}\},s_{\mathrm{cone}(Q)},t_{\mathrm{cone}(Q)}),

where
s_{\mathrm{cone}(Q)}
and
t_{\mathrm{cone}(Q)}
are extensions of
s_{Q}
and
t_{Q}
by
s_{Q}(a_{v})=v_{0}
and
t_{Q}(a_{v})=v
. We define by
i_{Q}\colon Q\hookrightarrow\mathrm{cone}(Q)
the corresponding embedding. If
m\colon Q\to Q^{\prime}
is a morphism of quivers, we get a canonical morphism
\mathrm{cone}(m)\colon\mathrm{cone}(Q)\to\mathrm{cone}(Q^{\prime})
.

Abusing the notations, if
a
is an arrow of
Q
, we also denote by
a\colon{\hbox to27.96pt{\vbox to3.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.59721pt\lower-1.79999pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{
\nullfont
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.59721pt}{-0.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\hookrightarrow Q
the corresponding morphism. We then introduce the usual notion of cones of diagrams by the following formula of arity
Q\times\mathrm{cone}(Q)
.

\mathrm{Cone}_{Q}(x,y)\colon\qquad\mathrm{restr}_{i_{Q}}(y)\approx x\quad\wedge\quad\bigwedge_{a\in A_{Q}}\mathrm{commute}(\mathrm{restr}_{\mathrm{cone}(a)}(y)).

Here is the notion of limit.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Limit}_{Q}(x,y)\colon\qquad

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cone}_{Q}(x,y)\quad\wedge

\displaystyle\qquad\forall z,\quad\mathrm{Cone}_{Q}(x,z)\ \to\ \exists!w,\ \mathrm{Cone}(y,w)\wedge\mathrm{restr}_{\mathrm{cone}(i_{Q})}(w)\approx z.

We also introduce the dual notion
\mathrm{Colimit}_{Q}\coloneqq\mathrm{Limit}_{Q^{\dagger}}^{\dagger}
.

The introduction of monos, epis, limits and colimits allows to state the axioms of abelian category given by Freyd
[
5
]
. First, we define zero objects and kernels as follows.

\displaystyle\mathrm{Zero}(x)\colon\qquad

\displaystyle\forall y,\quad\mathrm{Limit}_{\varnothing}(y,x)\ \wedge\ \mathrm{Colimit}_{\varnothing}(y,x),

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}(x,y)\colon\qquad

We also define
\mathrm{Coker}\coloneqq\mathrm{Ker}^{\dagger}
.

as the extension of

by the following formulas.

\displaystyle\mathrm{ZeroE}\colon

\displaystyle\mathrm{ProductE}\colon

\displaystyle\qquad\forall x,\exists y,\quad\mathrm{Limit}_{{\hbox to6.39pt{\vbox to2.14pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.77081pt\lower-1.06943pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{

\displaystyle\mathrm{CoproductE}\colon

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{ProductE}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\mathrm{KerE}\colon

\displaystyle\qquad\forall x,\exists y,\quad\mathrm{Ker}(x,y),

\displaystyle\mathrm{CokerE}\colon

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{KerE}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\mathrm{MonoNormal}\colon

\displaystyle\qquad\forall x,\quad\mathrm{Mono}(x)\ \to\ \exists y,\ \mathrm{Ker}(y,x),

\displaystyle\mathrm{EpiNormal}\colon

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{MonoNormal}^{\dagger}.

The following theorem states that
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ab}}
is a theory for diagrams over abelian categories.

Theorem 24
.

The categorical interpretation induced by any small abelian category is a model of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ab}}
. Conversely, any model of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ab}}
is isomorphic to the categorical interpretation associated to some small abelian category.

Proof.

This follows from
\cref
thm:category_theory and from
[
5
, Chapter 2]
.
∎

Proposition 25
.

The theory
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ab}}
implies its dual
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ab}}^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

The theory
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
implies its dual by
\cref
prop:Tcat_autodual. Moreover,
\mathrm{ZeroE}
clearly implies its dual. Finally, for the other axioms we added, we also added their dual.
∎

6
Decidability of the commerge problem

In this section, we use the notations of
\cref
ssec:quotient_category. Let
Q
be a quiver,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and, for each
i\in[k]
, let
Q_{i}
be a quiver and
m_{i}\colon Q_{i}\to Q
be a morphism. We define the following formula:

\mathrm{Commerge}_{m_{0},\dots,m_{k-1}}\colon\qquad\forall_{Q}x,\qquad\bigwedge_{i=0}^{k-1}\mathrm{commute}(\mathrm{restr}_{m_{i}}(x))\quad\to\quad\mathrm{commute}(x).

Definition 26
.

Notations as above, the
acyclic, resp. cyclic, commerge problem for morphisms, resp. embeddings,
m_{0},\dots,m_{k-1}
and for a theory
\mathcal{T}
is the problem of deciding the validity of
\mathrm{Commerge}_{m_{0},\dots,m_{k-1}}
among models of
\Sigma
, resp.
\mathring{\Sigma}
, verifying the theory
\mathcal{T}
.

We recall that a
thin category
is a category with at most one morphism between any pair of objects. Let
\mathrm{tot}_{Q_{i}}=\mathcal{BP}_{\!Q_{i}}
be the complete path relation on
Q_{i}
. Set
r_{i}\coloneqq m_{i\,*}(\mathrm{tot}_{Q_{i}})
for
i\in[k]
. Recall that
(r_{i})_{i\in[k]}
is the smallest path relation containing the
r_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
.

Lemma 27
.

Notation as above, the formula
\mathrm{Commerge}_{m_{0},\dots,m_{k-1}}
is valid among model of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
, resp.
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
, if and only if
{\langle Q\rangle}/(r_{i})_{i\in[k]}
is a thin category.

Proof.

Set
\mathcal{C}\coloneqq{\langle Q\rangle}/(r_{i})_{i\in[k]}
. It is a model of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
, resp.
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
. Moreover, the canonical diagram
D\colon{\langle Q\rangle}\to\mathcal{C}
verifies the premise of
\mathrm{Commerge}_{m_{0},\dots,m_{k-1}}
. If
\mathcal{C}
is not thin, then there ase two paths
p
and
q
in
{\langle Q\rangle}
with the same extremities which are not in relation. Then
\mathrm{comp}(p^{*}(D))
is the class of
p
in the quotient, which is different of the class of
q
, that is of
\mathrm{comp}(q^{*}(D))
. Hence
D
is not commutative.

For the other direction, by
\cref
thm:category_theory, it suffices to study diagrams in small categories. It is easy to check that any diagram
D^{\prime}
over
Q
in a category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
which verifies the condition of
\mathrm{Commerge}_{m_{0},\dots,m_{k-1}}
factors through
D
, i.e.,
D^{\prime}=\Psi\circ D
for some functor
\Psi\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. If
\mathcal{C}
is thin, then for any two paths
p
and
q
with same extremities in
Q
,

\mathrm{comp}(p^{*}(D^{\prime}))=\Psi(\mathrm{comp}(p^{*}(D)))=\Psi(\mathrm{comp}(q^{*}(D)))=\mathrm{comp}(q^{*}(D^{\prime})).

Hence
\mathrm{Commerge}_{m_{0},\dots,m_{k-1}}
is valid.
∎

Theorem 28
.

The acyclic commerge problem for
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
is decidable for any tuple of embeddings.

Proof.

By
\cref
lem:commerge_triviality, it suffices to decide if
{\langle Q\rangle}/(r_{i})_{i\in[k]}
. Since
{\langle Q\rangle}
and the
{\langle Q_{i}\rangle}
,
i\in[k]
are finite, we can compute relation
(m_{i\,*}(\mathrm{tot}_{Q_{i}})\mid i\in[k])
and check it is complete.
∎

Proposition 29
.

There exists a tuple of morphisms for which the cyclic commerge problem for
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
is undecidable.

Proof.

We proceed by reduction to an undecidability result, due to Markov
[
9
]
. For
B
an arbitrary finite set and
\langle B\rangle
the associated free monoid, let
M
be the finitely presentable monoid
\langle B\rangle/R
, for
R
a finite subset of
\langle B\rangle
. The triviality of
M
is undecidable.

Let
B
,
R
and
M
as above. Let
Q=(\{v\},B\sqcup\{e\},s_{Q},t_{Q})
be a quiver with one vertex and loops labeled by elements of
B
plus one loop
e
. To each element
\rho\in R
corresponds a path
p_{\rho}\colon\mathrm{PQ}_{k_{\rho}}\to Q
, for some

. Let

and
.
.
. Let

be the extension to

of

obtained by mapping the new arrow onto

. Also set

which maps the loop on

. We claim that the cyclic commerge problem for the

and
m_{e}
is undecidable. Indeed,

is the category associated to the monoid
M=\langle B\rangle/R
. Hence this category is thin if and only if the monoid is trivial. Markov Theorem
[

]
and

We strengthen the previous proposition to the case of embeddings.

Theorem 30
.

There exists a tuple of embeddings for which the cyclic commerge problem for
\mathring{\mathcal{T}}_{\mathrm{cat}}
is undecidable.

Proof.

Let
B
,
R
,
M
as in the proof of
\cref
prop:undecidable_commerge. Let
Q
be the quiver
(\{v\},B,s_{Q},t_{Q})
. If
k\geq 2
, we define the quiver
\mathring{Q}^{k}
as

\displaystyle\mathring{Q}^{k}\coloneqq\Big(\{v_{i}\mid i\in[k]\},\{b_{i,j}\mid b\in B,0\leq i<j<k\}\sqcup\{e_{i,j}\mid i,j\in[k]\},s_{\mathring{Q}^{k}},t_{\mathring{Q}^{k}}\Big)\text{ where}

\displaystyle s_{\mathring{Q}^{k}}(b_{i,j})=v_{i},\quad s_{\mathring{Q}^{k}}(e_{i,j})=v_{i},\quad t_{\mathring{Q}^{k}}(b_{i,j})=v_{j},\quad t_{\mathring{Q}^{k}}(e_{i,j})=v_{j}.

We have a projection
\pi:{\langle\mathring{Q}^{k}\rangle}\to{\langle Q\rangle}
, which maps
b_{i,j}
on
b
and
e_{i,j}
on
\mathrm{id}_{v}
, and a section
\iota:{\langle Q\rangle}\to{\langle\mathring{Q}^{k}\rangle}
defined by mapping
v
onto
v_{0}
and
b
onto
b_{0,k-1}\circ e_{k-1,0}
, where, as usual, we denote in a same way an arrow and the corresponding path of length one.

For
A
any subset of
A_{\mathring{Q}^{k}}
, let
m_{A}\colon\mathring{Q}^{k}|_{A}\hookrightarrow\mathring{Q}^{k}
be the canonical embedding, and let
r_{A}\coloneqq m_{A\,*}(\mathrm{tot}_{\mathring{Q}^{k}|_{A}})
. Set
r^{\prime}\coloneqq((r_{A})_{A\in\mathcal{A}})
for
\mathcal{A}\subset 2^{A_{\mathring{Q}^{k}}}
defined as the set containing

•

A_{e}\coloneqq\{e_{i,j}\mid i,j\in[k]\}
,

•

for
i<j
and
b\in B
,

A_{b,i,j}\coloneqq\{\underbrace{e_{0,i}}_{\text{if $i\neq 0$}},\ b_{i,j}\ ,\underbrace{e_{j,k-1}}_{\text{if $j\neq k-1$}},\ b_{0,k-1}\}.

We claim that
\pi
and
\iota
induce an equivalence of category between
{\langle Q\rangle}
and
{\langle\mathring{Q}^{k}\rangle}/r^{\prime}
. From the definition of
A_{e}
, for any
i,j,l\in[k]
, we have
e_{i,j}\circ e_{j,l}\sim e_{i,l}
and
e_{i,i}\sim\mathrm{id}_{i}
. Now the definition of
A_{b,i,j}
, for
b\in B
and
0\leq i<j<k
, induces that
e_{0,i}\circ b_{i,j}\circ e_{j,k-1}\sim b_{0,k-1}
. These relations generate all
r^{\prime}
, and they become equalities by applying the projection. Hence
\pi_{*}\colon{\langle\mathring{Q}^{k}\rangle}/r^{\prime}\to{\langle Q\rangle}
is well-defined. Clearly
\pi\circ\iota
is identity. Concerning the other direction, for
b\in B
and
0\leq i<j<k
, we have

\iota\circ\pi(b_{i,j})=b_{0,k-1}\circ e_{k-1,0}\sim e_{0,i}\circ b_{i,j}\circ e_{j,k-1}\circ e_{k-1,0}\sim e_{0,i}\circ b_{i,j}\circ e_{j,0}.

Hence we get a natural transformation
\eta
between the identity functor and
\iota\circ\pi_{*}
by setting
\eta_{i}\coloneqq e_{i,0}\in\mathrm{Hom}_{{\langle\mathring{Q}^{k}\rangle}/r^{\prime}}(i,\iota\circ\pi(i)=0)
. Since
e_{i,0}
is an isomorphism, we conclude that there is an equivalence of category between
{\langle Q\rangle}
and
{\langle\mathring{Q}^{k}\rangle}/r^{\prime}
.

Recall that
M
is the monoid
\langle B\rangle/R
. Assume that
k
is greater than the longest word in
R
. To any word
\rho=b^{1}b^{2}\dots b^{l}
in
R
corresponds a subset

A_{\rho}\coloneqq\{e_{0,l},b^{1}_{0,1},b^{2}_{1,2},\dots,b^{l}_{l-1,l}\}\subseteq A_{\mathring{Q}^{k}}.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{A}\cup\{A_{\rho}\mid\rho\in R\}
. We claim that
\mathrm{Commerge}_{(m_{A})_{A\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}}
is undecidable. Indeed,
\mathring{Q}^{k}/((r_{A})_{A\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}})
is equivalent as a category to
Q/(\pi_{*}(r_{A_{\rho}}))_{\rho\in R}
which is the category of the monoid
M
. Once again, we conclude using
\cref
lem:commerge_triviality and Markov Theorem.
∎

Theorem 31
.

The theory
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
contains undecidable formulas.

Proof.

Let
M
,
B
,
R
and
Q
as in the proof of
\cref
thm:undecidable_commerge_embeddings. Let
k
be the size of the longest word in
R
plus one. We define the quiver
Q^{k}
as

Q^{k}\coloneqq\Big(\{v_{i}\mid i\in[k]\},\{b_{i,j}\mid b\in B,0\leq i<j<k\}\sqcup\{e_{i,j}\mid 0\leq i<j<k\},s_{Q^{k}},t_{Q^{k}}\Big)

where

s_{Q^{k}}(b_{i,j})=v_{i},\quad s_{Q^{k}}(e_{i,j})=v_{i},\quad t_{Q^{k}}(b_{i,j})=v_{j},\quad t_{Q^{k}}(e_{i,j})=v_{j}.

Note that
Q^{k}
is acyclic. Let

\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq\{A_{b,i,j}\mid b\in B,0\leq i<j<k\}\ \cup\ \{A_{\rho}\mid\rho\in R\},

where
A_{b,i,j}
and
A_{\rho}
are defined as in the proof of
\cref
thm:undecidable_commerge_embeddings. For
A\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}
, let
m_{A}\colon Q^{k}|_{A}\hookrightarrow Q^{k}
be the corresponding embedding.
Consider the formula

\displaystyle\mathrm{CommergeWithId}_{M}\colon\qquad

\displaystyle\forall_{Q^{k}}x,\quad\bigwedge_{0\leq i<j<k}\mathrm{Id}(\mathrm{restr}_{v_{i}}(x),\mathrm{restr}_{e_{i,j}}(x))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\ \wedge\ \bigwedge_{A\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}}\mathrm{commute}(\mathrm{restr}_{A}(x))\quad\to\quad\mathrm{commute}(x).

The projection
\pi\colon Q^{k}\to Q
induces a pullback
\pi^{*}
between diagrams on
Q
and that on
Q^{k}
. It is easy to check that
\pi^{*}
induces a bijection between the diagrams on
Q
, verifying the condition of the commerge problem described in the proof of
\cref
prop:undecidable_commerge, and the diagrams on
Q^{k}
verifying the condition of
\mathrm{CommergeWithId}_{M}
. Moreover the bijection preserves commutativity. Hence we conclude the proof as for
\cref
prop:undecidable_commerge.
∎

7
Conclusion

We have shown that the many-sorted signature
\Sigma
is expressive enough to formulate a theory
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
and its extension
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{ab}}
, whose models are exactly and respectively diagrams in small categories, and that in small abelian categories. Restricting sorts to acyclic quivers makes the commerge problem for
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{cat}}
decidable. A companion file
[
1
]
to this submission illustrates how to implement a deep embedding of formulas of
\Sigma
using the
Coq
proof assistant
[
13
]
; its content should be easy to transpose to other proof systems. Theorem
duality_theorem_with_theory
shall bring a duality principle, i.e., that a formula of the language is valid if and only if its dual is valid, to any formalized definition of abelian categories.
\cref
thm:dec results in a complete decision procedure for commutativity clauses. The optimizations that make it work on concrete examples however go beyond the scope of the present article.

Similar concerns have motivated the implementation of the accomplished
Globular
proof assistant
[
2
]
, for higher-dimensional category theory. The closest related work we are aware of yet seem unpublished at the time of writing. Lafont’s categorical diagram editor
[
7
]
, based on the Unimath library
[
14
]
and Barras and Chabassier’s graphical interface for diagrammatic proofs
[
3
]
both provide a graphical interface for generating
Coq
proof scripts and visualizing
Coq
goals as diagrams. No specific automation is however provided. Himmel
[
6
]
describes a formalization of abelian categories in
Lean

[
4
]
, including proofs of the five lemma and of the snake lemma, and proof (semi-)automation tied to this specific formalization. Duality arguments are not addressed. Monbru
[
10
]
also discusses automation issues in diagram chases, and provides heuristics for generating them automatically, albeit expressed in a pseudo-language.
